"To me a country is like a woman--when sha loses
her virginity she is not a virgin ever agai’. For mo
when a country loses any part of her soverelenty she
is no more a nation. I would rather take the chance
on losing the coun try comple tely--seeing Lt sunk,
wrecked-——my family lost, my fortune ruined, than to try
to save her existence by Losing part of her independent
individuality by linking with a federation of countries-~
identifying herself as a part of a group--or by becoming
an economic semi-colony, taking her cue on how to trade
or produce from a big power. Let her be livaded, and go
down, fighting with honor, Let her perish from the face
of the earth. But never for a minuto should sho, through
fright and desire of protection or for the convenience
of a big power, give up one inch of har fOverelenty."

He went on to say that the very word federation meant elving up

one's complete Liberty to the common need for coming to common decisions and

therefore means loss of complete Liberty in self rule. Any plan of action

made for all has to include compromises from evoryone. Since twenty nations

of such varied types cannot live exactly by each other's me thod--it would
have to be a general melange of everything, and then none would be free to

use exactly his own particuler methods of governing and trade.

"Argentina can only exist as a nation by stending
alone," he said. "She Loses her identity as a nation
by connecting up with federations or submitting to a
big power. If shetgs taken by force she at least
sinks honestly. She mist, to stand alone, fortify

herself internally. We've got to find a way--economically....,
somehow,......."

When I arrived in Buenos Aires I found my old friend, Doctor Felipe
Espil, Argentine Ambassador to the United States, who told me that he had

been asked by hig Foreign Office what Washing ton generally and the Adminis-—

tration in particular thought of hig countrymen. "I told them," said he,

"that the opinion generally held in the States was that the Argentines were

Nazis." Last year I Spent ten weeks in Argentina and visited many of the

provinces of the country north of Tierra del Fuego. I was there two weeks

As communications with the Axis out of Chile as well as Argen-

tina has become such a controversial subject, J} think 1t would be well

to smplify this point. The Administration in Argentina make no attempt

to deny that messages zo out over tho reguler airways to the Axiy nations

in code. Messages also go out to the United Nations in code. They pro-

claim, however, inasmuch as the representatives in ow Stete Dopartment

find this to be the one point on which they condemn their neutral stand, =
the Foreign Office is looking into the possibility of placing a ban on

all code messages. I was told that Chile yeary ago sedopted such a measure

and they refer to this act as the precedent they would use as 4 basis
should they adopt the plan they now have undor consideration. Aside from
this one point, they wre unwilling to concede that the United States has
any logical besia for criticizing their neutral stand. On numerous
occasions I was reminded that the War hed been in progress for twenty-
eight months before we were attacked at Pearl Harbor; and that it was
their considered opinion had it not been for this treacherous attack the
United States would have remuined neutral in this War.

The Navel strength of Argentina is pathetic in comparison with
the coast line they have to defend. The Military strength at present con-
siste of 80,000 officers and men. As they have compulsory military train-
ing, 1t would be possible in a crisis to incresse the strength of the
Army up to around one million men, and the one thing Argentina is deter-
mined to do is to put up a defense aguinst any attempt to invade their

land no matter who the foe may be. There are over two hundred thousand

Germans living in the country, and I have given you a verbal report es
the plan of section with respect to this colony should the Axis ever @

an invasion.

The most pathetic attempt to stir up a favorable emotional reac-

tion is that which has been undertaken by the de Gaullists. In spite of

the almost universal love of France which exists in Argentina, the "Free
French" under the leadership of Bernard de Seiyes, have been able to accom—
plish exactly nothing. De Seiyes I understand, in spite of the role he is !
enacting, still maintains his connections with the Textile industry in
3: Lyons, and is still suspected of being one of the Directors of the Bank of
os France. Herold Horan scouted the last meeting of the de Gaullists, which
he told me was depressing as to size and enthusiesm, and that the Jewish
race accounted for over 90% of these present.

The British UColony in Argentina is by far the most important.
They own the railroads and furnish all the coal; they own the public utili-
ties, the "undergrounds" in Buenos Aires—-their real estate holdings are
enormous and they provide a substantial portion of the money loaned on
Estanciss. They are extremely popular with ‘ne Argentines. Senora Santa-
marina, wife of the President of the Bank of the Nation, told me frankly
a year ago that the Argentines didn't mind having the British es their
a mastere, but didn't believe they would relish a change. She went on to
a explain that the credit which the United States had extended to the
: y Argentine consisting of $116,000,000 was handed out so freely as to make
them suspicious of what we might demand in return for it. I notice that

our Under-Secretery of State announced last week that this credit had been

¢

cancelled. On the other hand, I learned while in Argentina that not one:cen a

of it had ever been used. The British however, are buying beef and 9

Congressman Hamilton Fish. I have twice discreetly b

" I have only twice in my life mot Hamilton
Fish, when we could sit and talk at length. JI have
met him casually at many sootall affatrs in Washington
when Mrs Thomsen and I were not soolal lepers. Mrg,. Thon-

years before that by Borehers predecessor. Borchers merely
continued a lease of a suitable house for the residence
of our Consul. General"

speaking his own mind.'

December 8th, 1941

DEAR COLONEL DONOVAN:

On Friday afternoon, December 5th, 1941, DR. Hans
Thomsen came to my home in New York for dinner. I also
spent Saturday morning with him. During our conversation
of several hours in all, he said the following items
which I think may be of interest to the Administration:

"Japan and the United States were near a friendly
solution of the Far Fastern problem, when the Chinese
stepped in and barred it."

"Me Japanese and the Chinese ars nogotiating
right now between each other. They wish to settle their
affairs between themselves without help or obstacles from
other countries. When they do finally compose their
difficulties, it will be done by them, between themselves,
without outside interference."

"It is not to Germany's advantage to have Japan
go to war with the United States, as this would invoive
Germany in formal war with the United States. Germany's
purpose is just as well served by a state of high tension
in the Pacific."

"The main interest of the United States in the Burma
Road is the tungsten which comes out of China on return
bound trucks. The munitions of war sent in to Chungking
are not sufficient in volume, and cannot ever be, to do
more than keep CHina barely going. They are not sufficient
to bring China victory over Japan. No heavy munitions
are now going to Chungking from Russia."

"If war should come between Japan and the United

States, it will be found that Siberia has been dangerously
denuded of Russian troops. The Rostov offensive by the
Russians wes fought by twenty of the best divisions of the
Siberian army, taken from vital Siberian points, to
strengthen Timoshenko's army. Japan will now have no
difficulty in overwhelming the meritime area of Siberia

if they decide to move there. Incidentally, the United
State has asked for a naval and eir base on the coast

of Siberia. Jepan will never permit this."

MALCOLM R. LOVELL
26 EAST 36% STREET
NEW YORK

September 25th, I94I

Colonel William J. Donovan
Bureau of Co-ordination of Infornation

Washington, D.C.

My dear Colonel Donovan,

I have given mich thought
to ways in which I can be of hel) to you. Since
our mesting of last Monday I have tried to place
in order, the problems involved.

Since my assistance to you
requires co-operation on your part may I outline
my problem as I see it,

As I have told you, I now
consider the United States no longer to be at peace.
We may not legally be at war, but actually we are,
It therefore follows, to me, that. I may not prop-
erly pursue the efforts for peace on which I have
so consistently labored during these past three
years. I feel that I may now logically do one of
two things,- "stay in the quiet", as Quakers say,
and meticulously avoid all the Germans, Frenchmen,
and Britishers I have made friends of during the
above period. It seems to me I should also avoid
my Anerican friends who are accustomed to discuss
international affaireg and the relation of the
United States to world problems. My exceptionally
Olose friendship with Dr. Hans Thomsen, and
efforts over the years to persuads him and his
government to pursue paths of lovs rather than
hatred, logically and realistically lay me open
to suspicion in this day when war is really with us.

This friendship naturally
leads even men of great wisdom and charity, suoh
as yourself, to fear that I might, now that the
United States is at war, Give comfort and aid in

Dear Bill:

If you are at all interested in the development of really up-
to-date and functional uniforms for speciulized attack uniforms,

Jean and I send you our best regards.

Sincerely yours,

U. S. Maval Hospital,

Washington, D.C.

Dear Bill;-

My son Louis, a graduate of Holy Cross College
and Fordham Univeraity Law School » insists upoh ay writing
to you.

He was rejected for selective service and placed
in class 4F, because of a ruptured ear drum. He is in no
wise deaf or otherwise incapacitated by the condition,

He enlisted in the 69th Regiment, New York Quard
but now feels very strongly that he should be in more active
service and feels that you might find a place for him, some-
where or anywhere.

\

He has had an application with the F.B.I. for
several months but has heard nothing from it,

a With best regards from all my officer personnel,

My dear B11);

Please pardon the penoil, stationery, ete,,
it X am writing thie letter on the edge of my bed,

Tam in a new camp which as yet has absolutely no
facilities as yet. However it 4s building very rapidly
iand will be a great place ina few weeks,

I arrived here Friday and my captain
informed me today that he wanted me for permanent party
and that he would recommend me for Officers Tradning
Camp. Me intimated that he would like to do 1t immed-
lately 1€ 4¢ were possible for him te do so, but he cannot
do it in accordanes with Camp regulations, so I am witlering
if you could secure this for me without my going thru
thie wait,

I am confident I wili acquit myself oreditably
and if you will seoure this appointment for me, 1 will
Greatly appreotate it and I assure you I will not let you
down; Af I cannot secure the appointment this way it
my be necessary for me to wait many months ag tT am now
Applying from Utah which only had 17 vacancies last week
and with the large number of men here 1t #urely will have
considerable sompetition,

I have exhausted every means at my command to sedure a oan
mission in either the Navy, the Army, or the Marine Corps. Tf have
also tried to enliet in the Navy, but until reoently wae unable to
do so ae I was not a specialiat dn any partiouler Cleld.

Mowever, within the paat Cow weekp the Navy hae relaxed their
regulations and Ioan reiniist in the Navy with the same rating I
held during the last wer,

When I enlisted last time I had impacted wisdom teeth, and as
@ result I wae compelled to have acme of my teeth extracted and bridges
made to replace them. Over a period of twenty-five years these bridges
naturally deteriorated and it was necessary for me to have all of my
tooth reaved, Last week I tried to enlist in the Army but was turned
down because of the fact that my lower jaw had not healed oul fielently
and the denture did not £1t properly as a result of thie oomlition.s f
would not like to be turned down by the Navy, therefore, I did not make
application for enlistment until I had an opportunity to write to you.
If you oan possibly do it, I would appreodate it if you would take up
with some officer in the Navy Department the poswibility of my seouring
a waiver for my defective dental vondition.s My ruting wae RM20, and I
eannot see how this would interfere with the performance of my duty.

In addition if it would be possible for you to help me get a
better rating than seoond class, I would appreciate it as I need all
finanojial aid in supporting my family thet I oan seoure, an I feel
that I oan hold dow a Chiefte rating satisfactorily. If you oan do
this for me, I will appreciate a vopy of the letter, so that I oan

.., predent “it when I try to reinliet in the Navy.

Dear Bills

I am in receipt of your letter of December
18th, in reply to my letter of December 7th. Several
days after I wrote you, I applied for a commission at
the Naval Reserve Headquarters here, but I was informed
that I could not qualify as I did not have a college
diploma, nor was I qualified as a purolmsing agent or
in eome other specialised line of endeavor -- my ex-
perience being along exeoutive lines.

Your letter was greatly appreciated, and
if conditions change and you are able at a future date
to be of assistance to me in seouring this oommission,
I will be grateful for anything you oan do for me.

Very beat personal regards and wishing you
e Merry Christmas and a Happy New Year, I am,

Sinoerely yours,

li. FUNCTIONS OF OFFICE OF STRATEGIC SERVICES

(Ref. - J.P.W.C. 45/D)

GENSRAL STRONG pointed out that in connection .
with paragraph 5 of the directive it would appear that the
study required will involve some investigation of the pro-
pagandsa activities of the Office of War Information. COLONEL
DONOVAN agreed that the Committee could probably make re-
commendations on the basis of its findings; he was supported
in this view by Admiral Train.

——— COLONEL DONOVAN suggested that instead of refer-
s: ring this directive to the Sudcommittee, it would be highly
, desirable for two representativas of this Committee to visit

the Office of Strategic Services and examine in person its
personnel and operations. After discussion, GENERAL STRONG
and ADMIRAL TRAIN agreed to visit the 0.S.S., on condition
that General Wedemeyer, Captain Pickhardt and Captain Grosskopf
would prepare the Committee's recommendations.

GENERAL WEDEMBYER requested guidance on certain
parts of tne directive, especially paragraph 3 pertaining to
intelligence functions. GENERAL STRONG stated that a great

many of the intelligence functions performed by the 0.S.S.

i are direct duplications of similar functions performed by

E O.N.I. and M.I.S., dut that there are certain fields in which
' the latter are limited. COLONEL DONOVAN stated that there
was no desire in 0.S.S. to duplicate activities of O.N.I.

and H.IF.S., and outlined the history of conversations with
General Miles, Admiral Kirk and representatives of the F.B.1I.
which resulted in the 0.5.5. taking over certain intelligence
activities, This agreoment on those functions was later
approved by the Secretary of War, the Sesretary of Nevy, and
the President.

COLONEL DONOVAN pointed out that the first memorandum
enclosed in J.P.W.C,. 21/1 had not yet been acted on by the Subcom-
mittee but that the second memorandum had been approved by the
Subcommittee with recommendation that the 416 grades and ratings
be furnished to the 0.S.S. He explained that there is presently
& Shortage of enlisted men for administration, security, and in-
structional duties at the eight school - camps, and that because
of that shortage only four of these camps are now operating.

LT. COL. WILLIAMS described the functions of each of
the schools and the type of instruction being given in them,
About two hundred instructors and students have been trained so
far and about sixty students are now in the schools. The system
is parallel to that usad by the British and in fact all the in- ;
structors have gone through British schools.

GENERAL STRONG suggested that the "Propaganda School"
be renamed "The Personnel Relations School" in order to avoid any
Seeming duplication of the functions of the O.W.I.

COMMANDER VANDERBILT outlined the mothods of reoruit-
ing and investigations made for new studonts, indicating that the
Army and Navy have been drawn upon as well as some personnel from
thea foraign grouos of the British Army.

LT. COLONEL WILLIAMS also discussed tho bases of oper-
ations and the allocations of agents to various countries. An
agroamant has been made to supplement the British effort in these
speclal activities, so that if the United States takes over an

areas the British can turn over their organization to the U. 8S.
command, ami vice versa.

COLONEL DONOVAN stated that the 416 grades and rates
requested were the minimum to ostablish the schools on an onerat-
ing basts, but that Lt will take oare of future expansion for the
schools for subversive training only and not for any expansion
for training guerrilla groups. It was not considered foasible to A

use elvilians for the duties for which Army personnel had beon
raquagtod,
